# Run iterators: loops in each function instead of loop macros

Today the loops that walk arrays live in macros: `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()`, `RRAY_BINARY()` and `RRAY_REDUCE()`. Each operation passes in a one-element kernel (`ONE`). Anything an operation needs beyond that one-element kernel, like a 64-bit total or a pairwise sum, has to become a new macro parameter or a new macro. `plans/accuracy.md` shows how quickly that grows.

This plan looks at the alternative: an iterator that the operation drives itself, with the loops written in each function. We built three iterator designs into a variant of rray4 and benchmarked them against the current macros on broadcasting, addition and sums.

---

# Summary

- A per-element iterator (a `next()` that hands back `x` and `out` locations for every element) is simplest to use, but too slow. It's up to 7x slower on sums, 2x to 2.7x slower on double addition, and 1.1x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting.

- An iterator that hands back one run at a time, used with a single loop over the run, matches the macros when every operand moves along the run. When one operand stays fixed, it's 1.3x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting and addition, and 4x to 7x slower on sums.

- The same run iterator, with each function writing one loop per stride case (an operand fixed or moving), matches the macros in nearly every case we measured. The exception is very short runs: adding `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` (a million runs of 10 elements) is 4% to 6% slower, in every one of six runs. It's faster in one case: summing `[10, 1e6]` over axis 1 takes 2.75 ms against 3.53 ms, because the total stays in a local variable.

- That design is what NumPy does. It is also what the macros already do. The loops are the same, so the speed is the same. What changes is where the loops live: in each function, where they can be read and changed, instead of inside a macro.

- One run iterator can serve any number of inputs, replacing today's separate one-input and two-input plans. It's as fast as the specialized ones, as long as each function passes the number of inputs as a literal (`1`, `2`) and the call is inlined. With the count known only at runtime, short runs get 25% to 75% slower.

- The short-run gap goes away when the iterator does less per run: keep one location array that the iterator advances directly (no per-run copy for the caller), and advance at the end of each run instead of checking for the first run at the start, like the macros do. That version is within 2.5% of the macro on every short-run case.

- Keep the plan and the iterator as separate structs. A merged struct is just as fast when it's built inside the kernel. But when the driver builds it and the kernel uses it through a pointer, raw broadcasting gets up to 5x slower and a strided mean 11% slower. With a small `const` plan passed in and the iterator built locally in the kernel, the fast version is the default, with no rule to remember.

- The cost is repetition. rray4 has 84 `RRAY_BINARY()` calls, 32 `RRAY_REDUCE()` calls and 5 `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` calls. Writing every stride case out by hand in each of them is a lot of code. How to handle the per-type repetition is the main open question.

Recommendation: adopt one run iterator for any number of inputs, in the "advance at the end" shape, with per-stride-case loops written in each function. Decide separately how to avoid writing every type out by hand.

---

# How it works today

`src/strided-iterator.h` builds a plan from a point space (the dimensions being walked) and a subspace (the strides of the array being read or written). After merging axes that can be walked as one ("coalescing"), the first axis is walked as one run, and later axes advance between runs with `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT()`.

The header documents three things that matter here:

- "For performance and flexibility, the user is responsible for managing the run loop along the first axis. We've tried many alternative approaches but they tend to tank performance quickly as you increase the level of abstractions."

- The plan holds only fixed state. The positions that change are kept by the caller, so the compiler can keep them in registers.

- When a stride along the run is 0, the macros take a separate path where the compiler can see the location doesn't change. It then loads the fixed value once and vectorizes the rest.

Each macro owns the full walk: allocate the output, set up the plan, loop over runs, branch on the stride cases, call `ONE` per element, and advance between runs. A few functions (`combine.c`, `index.c`, `permute-axes.c`, `split.c`) already write their own loops against the plan directly, without a macro.

---

# What NumPy does

This section is from knowledge of NumPy's source, not checked against it. NumPy isn't installed on the benchmark machine.

- One iterator, `nditer`, for everything. It reorders axes so the inner loop follows memory order, merges axes that can be walked as one, and hands out one run at a time: a pointer, a length, and a stride for each operand. For reductions, it walks the input in memory order no matter which axis is reduced.

- Reductions reuse the elementwise kernel. `np.add.reduce` calls the same inner loop as `np.add`, with operands `(out, in, out)`. When a whole run feeds one output, the output's stride is 0.

- Each typed inner loop checks its strides and picks a path. The `float64` add loop first checks `IS_BINARY_REDUCE` (output stride 0, output is also the first input). If so, it runs a pairwise sum over the run into one local total. Otherwise it picks a fast path for contiguous operands, a fixed scalar operand, and so on, and uses SIMD there.

- SIMD is written by hand with NumPy's own layer (and Highway for newer kernels), compiled for several CPU levels and picked when NumPy loads. rray4 can only rely on the compiler vectorizing at R's default `-O2`, since CRAN doesn't allow `-march=native`.

- When data needs a type conversion or is misaligned, `nditer` copies it in chunks (8192 elements by default) into a contiguous buffer, and the inner loop runs on that.

- The typed inner loops are generated, not written by hand: once from `.c.src` templates expanded by a Python script, now mostly C++ templates. There is one loop body per operation, expanded across types. The iterator never knows what operation it's running, and the inner loop never knows how many dimensions the array has.

So NumPy uses the "run iterator, per-stride-case loops" design, and avoids writing every type out by generating the per-type loops.

---

# The iterators

Both iterators wrap an existing `rray_strided_iterator_plan` (one subspace) or `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` (two subspaces). They hold the changing positions in a struct on the caller's stack. Because `next()` is `static inline` and the struct never leaves the function, the compiler keeps its fields in registers, so the header's concern about positions in registers doesn't apply. That held for every case below, with Apple clang 17. GCC is not checked.

## Run iterator

`next()` advances one run. The caller gets the run's range in the point space (`start` to `end`) and, for each subspace, where the run starts (`loc`) and how far to move per element (`stride`).

```c
struct rray_run_iterator {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize loc;
  r_ssize stride;

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize run_size;
  r_ssize loc_start;
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan;
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
};

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan
) {
  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.plan = plan;
  it.size = rray_strided_iterator_plan_size(plan);
  it.run_size = rray_strided_iterator_plan_run_size(plan);
  it.stride = rray_strided_iterator_plan_run_stride(plan);

  it.start = 0;
  it.end = 0;
  it.loc = 0;
  it.loc_start = 0;

  rray_strided_iterator_plan_point_init(plan, it.v_point);

  return it;
}

static inline bool rray_run_iterator_next(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  if (it->end == it->size) {
    return false;
  }

  if (it->end != 0) {
    const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan = it->plan;
    RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT(it->loc_start, it->v_point, plan);
  }

  it->start = it->end;
  it->end = it->start + it->run_size;
  it->loc = it->loc_start;

  return true;
}
```

`rray_run_iterator2` is the same, with `loc1`, `loc2`, `stride1` and `stride2`, built on `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` and `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT2()`. The full code is in the appendix.

The meaning of the two spaces depends on the operation, as it does for the plan today:

- Broadcasting and binary operations walk the output. `start` to `end` indexes the output, and `loc` indexes the input.

- Reductions walk the input. `start` to `end` indexes the input, and `loc` indexes the output.

## Element iterator

`next()` advances one element and hands back `index` (in the point space) and `loc` (in the subspace). It starts at `loc = -stride` and `run_i = -1`, so the first call goes through the same "next element in this run" path as every other call, without a special first branch. The full code is in the appendix. It is here as the baseline that was ruled out.

---

# Worked examples

All three use the run iterator with a loop per stride case. These are the `run_split` variants from the benchmarks, exactly as they were compiled.

## Broadcast

One input, walking the output. When the input's stride is 0, the run repeats one value.

```c
static r_obj* rray_broadcast_dbl(
  r_obj* x,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan
) {
  const r_ssize size = rray_strided_iterator_plan_size(plan);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(size));
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);

  struct rray_run_iterator it = rray_run_iterator(plan);

  while (rray_run_iterator_next(&it)) {
    if (it.stride == 0) {
      const double x_elt = v_x[it.loc];

      for (r_ssize i = it.start; i < it.end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = x_elt;
      }
    } else {
      r_ssize x_loc = it.loc;

      for (r_ssize i = it.start; i < it.end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = v_x[x_loc];
        x_loc += it.stride;
      }
    }
  }

  FREE(1);
  return out;
}
```

For example, broadcasting `[1, 4000]` to `[4000, 4000]` gives 4000 runs of 4000 elements, each with input stride 0. Every run takes the first branch and fills with one value. Broadcasting `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]` gives 4000 runs with input stride 1, each copying the same 4000 contiguous values.

## Add

Two inputs, walking the output. Each input is either fixed or moving along the run, so there are four cases. The integer version passes `error_call` to its one-element kernel directly, with no `RRAY_BINARY_ARGS()` needed.

```c
static r_obj* rray_add_int_int(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* y,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator2_plan* plan,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  const r_ssize size = rray_strided_iterator2_plan_size(plan);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(size));

Bash: cat src/strided-iterator2.h src/binary.h src/decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h 
cat src/strided-iterator2.h src/binary.h src/decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h
#ifndef RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR2_H
#define RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR2_H

#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "rlang.h"
#include "size.h"

#include "decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h"

// Strided iterator plan
//
// Optimizes and assists in walking a multidimensional point space defined by
// `v_dimensions`. As it walks, it updates a user provided `start` in an
// alternate subspace defined by `v_strides`.
//
// For performance and flexibility, the user is responsible for managing the run
// loop along the first axis. We've tried many alternative approaches but they
// tend to tank performance quickly as you increase the level of abstractions.
//
// The plan holds only immutable state. The positions that change as it walks
// are managed by the caller. This lets the compiler keep these positions in
// registers, which has significant performance implications.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Examples
//
// For broadcasting, the dimensions you broadcast to make up the larger point
// space. This is walked in order. The original dimensions of the array make
// up the subspace. So as you walk the output's point space you can create
// `location`s back into your original array to pull from.
//
// For reducing, it's actually a special form of broadcasting. The original
// dimensions of the array are the point space. The reduced dimensions are the
// subspace. So as you walk the original array, you can create `location`s into
// the output to accumulate the reduced result at.
//
// For permuting axes, the permuted dimensions make up the point space. The
// original dimensions of the array make up the subspace. So as you walk the
// output's point space you can create `location`s back into your original
// array to pull from.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Optimization - First axis runs
//
// After coalescing, the iterator walks the entire first axis in one inner run
// while holding all later axes fixed. It only updates the later point
// coordinates between runs, rather than checking and carrying them after every
// element. This gives the compiler a small loop where the index and locations
// advance by fixed strides, making it much easier to optimize and vectorize.
//
// Practical examples of when this is useful:
//
// - Identically shaped binary array operations. Adding two [2, 4, 5] arrays
//   coalesces to dimensions [40], so the entire operation is one first axis run
//   where both input locations advance contiguously.
//
// - Broadcasting over a later axis. Broadcasting a [2, 3] array to [2, 3, 4]
//   coalesces to dimensions [6, 4] with location strides [1, 0]. The iterator
//   performs four first axis runs of size 6, copying six contiguous values
//   before updating the later axis.
//
// - Reducing over a later axis. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array over its third axis
//   also coalesces to dimensions [6, 4] with output strides [1, 0]. Each first
//   axis run accumulates one contiguous slice into six output locations before
//   advancing along the reduced axis.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Optimization - Coalescing
//
// Coalescing axes is an important optimization used to reduce the number of
// axes we have to iterate over by "merging" adjacent compatible ones. This can
// improve performance by 3-5x on its own in some cases.
//
// Practical examples of when this is useful:
//
// - Identically shaped binary array operations. Adding two arrays with
//   dimensions [2, 4, 5] gives both inputs strides [1, 2, 8]. All adjacent axes
//   coalesce (2 * 1 = 2, 4 * 2 = 8) into one dimension [40] with stride [1],
//   resulting in one flat vectorizable loop.
//
// - Scalar broadcasting across an entire array. Adding a scalar to a [2, 4, 5]
//   array gives the array strides [1, 2, 8] and the scalar strides [0, 0, 0].
//   Both coalesce completely, producing a [40] loop with array stride [1] and
//   scalar stride [0].
//
// - Contiguous adjacent axes within a broadcast operation. Broadcasting a
//   [2, 3] array over point dimensions [2, 3, 4] produces location strides
//   [1, 2, 0]. The adjacent axes 1 and 2 coalesce (2 * 1 = 2) into size 6,
//   leaving dimensions [6, 4] and strides [1, 0].
//
// - Contiguous regions within a reduction. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array over its
//   third axis maps into a [2, 3] result using location strides [1, 2, 0].
//   Coalescing produces dimensions [6, 4], making it the same as the broadcast
//   example above.
//
// - Point-space dimensions of size 1. Output point dimensions of [1, 3, 4] can
//   have strides [0, 1, 3]. The dimension 1 first axis is absorbed into the
//   dimension 3 second axis, adopting stride 1, after which the dimension 4
//   third axis also coalesces. The result is one dimension [12] with stride
//   [1]. Note that this isn't broadcasting. This is when both input and output
//   have an axis that stays dimension 1, which is somewhat rare.
//
// For iterator2, note that both sets of location strides must be coalescible,
// as coalescing changes the output dimensionality, so it's all or nothing.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Optimization - Fixed zero stride paths
//
// After coalescing, the first axis is walked by the inner loop. A stride of 0
// on this axis means that a subspace location stays fixed while the point space
// moves along it. Broadcasting uses this to reuse an input value. Reducing uses
// it to accumulate into the same output location. A zero stride on a later axis
// does not use this path because later axes advance between inner runs.
//
// When the inner loop receives the stride as a runtime value, the compiler
// can't prove that the location is fixed and falls back to a scalar loop. The
// public iteration macros check for a zero stride and pass a literal 0 to a
// specialized path. This lets the compiler see that the location does not
// change. For binary operations, it can then hoist the fixed load out of the
// loop and vectorize the remaining stride 1 work.
//
// Practical examples of when this is useful:
//
// - Scalar broadcasting across an entire array. Adding a scalar to a [2, 4, 5]
//   array coalesces to point dimensions [40], with array strides [1] and scalar
//   strides [0]. The scalar location stays fixed while the array and output
//   advance contiguously. The same path is used by arithmetic, comparison,
//   equality, and extrema operations.
//
// - Row broadcasting within a matrix. Adding a [1, 4] row to a [2, 4] array
//   gives the array strides [1, 2] and row strides [0, 1]. Each inner run
//   therefore reuses one row value while the array and output advance
//   contiguously.
//
// - Higher dimensional row broadcasting. Adding a [1, 3, 4] array to a
//   [2, 3, 4] array coalesces to dimensions [2, 12], with array strides [1, 2]
//   and broadcast strides [0, 1]. Each of the 12 inner runs uses the fixed
//   path.
//
// - Shared leading dimensions of size 1. Adding [1, 1, 4] to [1, 3, 4]
//   absorbs the shared first axis and produces dimensions [3, 4], with first
//   input strides [0, 1] and second input strides [1, 3].
//
// - Reducing over the first axis. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array to [1, 3, 4]
//   produces output strides [0, 1, 3], which coalesce to dimensions [2, 12]
//   with output strides [0, 1]. Each inner run accumulates into one fixed
//   output location.
struct rray_run_iterator {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];

  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int dimensionality;

  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
};

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides,
  r_ssize n
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
    r_stop_internal(
      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
      n,
      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
    );
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    r_lazy_null
  );

  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
  }

  r_memcpy(
    it.v_strides,
    v_strides,
    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)
  );

  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(

308 more lines cut

Bash: sed -n 1,80p plans/iterators.md | tail -60; grep -n "^#\+ " plans/iterators.md 
sed -n 1,80p plans/iterators.md | tail -60; grep -n "^#\+ " plans/iterators.md
- The short-run gap goes away when the iterator does less per run: keep one location array that the iterator advances directly (no per-run copy for the caller), and advance at the end of each run instead of checking for the first run at the start, like the macros do. That version is within 2.5% of the macro on every short-run case.

- Keep the plan and the iterator as separate structs. A merged struct is just as fast when it's built inside the kernel. But when the driver builds it and the kernel uses it through a pointer, raw broadcasting gets up to 5x slower and a strided mean 11% slower. With a small `const` plan passed in and the iterator built locally in the kernel, the fast version is the default, with no rule to remember.

- The cost is repetition. rray4 has 84 `RRAY_BINARY()` calls, 32 `RRAY_REDUCE()` calls and 5 `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` calls. Writing every stride case out by hand in each of them is a lot of code. How to handle the per-type repetition is the main open question.

Recommendation: adopt one run iterator for any number of inputs, in the "advance at the end" shape, with per-stride-case loops written in each function. Decide separately how to avoid writing every type out by hand.

---

# How it works today

`src/strided-iterator.h` builds a plan from a point space (the dimensions being walked) and a subspace (the strides of the array being read or written). After merging axes that can be walked as one ("coalescing"), the first axis is walked as one run, and later axes advance between runs with `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT()`.

The header documents three things that matter here:

- "For performance and flexibility, the user is responsible for managing the run loop along the first axis. We've tried many alternative approaches but they tend to tank performance quickly as you increase the level of abstractions."

- The plan holds only fixed state. The positions that change are kept by the caller, so the compiler can keep them in registers.

- When a stride along the run is 0, the macros take a separate path where the compiler can see the location doesn't change. It then loads the fixed value once and vectorizes the rest.

Each macro owns the full walk: allocate the output, set up the plan, loop over runs, branch on the stride cases, call `ONE` per element, and advance between runs. A few functions (`combine.c`, `index.c`, `permute-axes.c`, `split.c`) already write their own loops against the plan directly, without a macro.

---

# What NumPy does

This section is from knowledge of NumPy's source, not checked against it. NumPy isn't installed on the benchmark machine.

- One iterator, `nditer`, for everything. It reorders axes so the inner loop follows memory order, merges axes that can be walked as one, and hands out one run at a time: a pointer, a length, and a stride for each operand. For reductions, it walks the input in memory order no matter which axis is reduced.

- Reductions reuse the elementwise kernel. `np.add.reduce` calls the same inner loop as `np.add`, with operands `(out, in, out)`. When a whole run feeds one output, the output's stride is 0.

- Each typed inner loop checks its strides and picks a path. The `float64` add loop first checks `IS_BINARY_REDUCE` (output stride 0, output is also the first input). If so, it runs a pairwise sum over the run into one local total. Otherwise it picks a fast path for contiguous operands, a fixed scalar operand, and so on, and uses SIMD there.

- SIMD is written by hand with NumPy's own layer (and Highway for newer kernels), compiled for several CPU levels and picked when NumPy loads. rray4 can only rely on the compiler vectorizing at R's default `-O2`, since CRAN doesn't allow `-march=native`.

- When data needs a type conversion or is misaligned, `nditer` copies it in chunks (8192 elements by default) into a contiguous buffer, and the inner loop runs on that.

- The typed inner loops are generated, not written by hand: once from `.c.src` templates expanded by a Python script, now mostly C++ templates. There is one loop body per operation, expanded across types. The iterator never knows what operation it's running, and the inner loop never knows how many dimensions the array has.

So NumPy uses the "run iterator, per-stride-case loops" design, and avoids writing every type out by generating the per-type loops.

---

# The iterators

Both iterators wrap an existing `rray_strided_iterator_plan` (one subspace) or `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` (two subspaces). They hold the changing positions in a struct on the caller's stack. Because `next()` is `static inline` and the struct never leaves the function, the compiler keeps its fields in registers, so the header's concern about positions in registers doesn't apply. That held for every case below, with Apple clang 17. GCC is not checked.

## Run iterator

`next()` advances one run. The caller gets the run's range in the point space (`start` to `end`) and, for each subspace, where the run starts (`loc`) and how far to move per element (`stride`).

+r_obj* rray_binary_arithmetic_run(
+  r_obj* x,
+  r_obj* y,
+  rray_binary_arithmetic_run_switch_fn fn_switch,
+  struct rray_arg* x_arg,
+  struct rray_arg* y_arg,
+  struct r_lazy error_call
+) {
+  check_unclassed(x, x_arg, error_call);
+  check_unclassed(y, y_arg, error_call);
+
+  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, x_arg, error_call));
+  y = KEEP(arg_as_array(y, y_arg, error_call));
+
+  const rray_binary_arithmetic_run_fn fn =
+    fn_switch(x, y, x_arg, y_arg, error_call);
+
+  r_obj* x_dimensions = r_dim(x);
+  r_obj* y_dimensions = r_dim(y);
+
+  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);
+  const int* v_y_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(y_dimensions);
+
+  const int x_dimensionality =
+    rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);
+  const int y_dimensionality =
+    rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(y_dimensions);
+
+  r_obj* dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions2(
+    v_x_dimensions,
+    x_dimensionality,
+    v_y_dimensions,
+    y_dimensionality,
+    x_arg,
+    y_arg,
+    error_call
+  ));
+  const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions);
+  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(dimensions);
+  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);
+
+  r_ssize v_x_broadcast_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_dimensions(
+    v_x_dimensions,
+    x_dimensionality,
+    dimensionality,
+    v_x_broadcast_strides
+  );
+
+  r_ssize v_y_broadcast_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_dimensions(
+    v_y_dimensions,
+    y_dimensionality,
+    dimensionality,
+    v_y_broadcast_strides
+  );
+
+  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * 2];
+  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    v_strides[axis * 2] = v_x_broadcast_strides[axis];
+    v_strides[axis * 2 + 1] = v_y_broadcast_strides[axis];
+  }
+
+  r_obj* out =
+    KEEP(fn(x, y, v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides, error_call));
+  r_attrib_poke_dim(out, dimensions);
+
+  r_obj* out_names = KEEP(rray_broadcast_names2(x, y, dimensions));
+
+  if (out_names != r_null) {
+    r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out, out_names);
+  }
+
+  FREE(5);
+  return out;
+}

Bash: sed -n 33,70p src/strides.c; grep -rn "rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_arrays" src/*.c 
sed -n 33,70p src/strides.c; grep -rn "rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_arrays" src/*.c
// For use with strided iterator n plan
void rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_arrays(
  r_obj* const* v_froms,
  r_ssize froms_size,
  int to_dimensionality,
  r_ssize* v_out
) {
  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < froms_size; ++i) {
    r_obj* from_dimensions = r_dim(v_froms[i]);
    const int* v_from_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(from_dimensions);
    const int from_dimensionality =
      rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(from_dimensions);

    r_ssize stride = 1;

    for (int axis = 0; axis < to_dimensionality; ++axis) {
      const int dimension =
        axis < from_dimensionality ? v_from_dimensions[axis] : 1;
      v_out[(r_ssize) axis * froms_size + i] = dimension == 1 ? 0 : stride;
      stride *= dimension;
    }
  }
}
src/index.c:66:  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_arrays(
src/strides.c:34:void rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_arrays(

Yes, I think it's a good change. Today rray_binary_arithmetic_run() computes the x and y broadcast strides, interleaves them into v_strides, and passes that buffer through to every kernel. The kernel passes it straight to rray_run_iterator() and does nothing else with it. Your helper moves that setup to the one place that uses it. The stride layout ([dimensionality][n], axis-major) then stays inside strided-iterator2.h and stops being part of the kernel signature.

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_binary_run_iterator(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const int* v_x_dimensions,
  int x_dimensionality,
  const int* v_y_dimensions,
  int y_dimensionality
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  r_ssize v_x_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_dimensions(...);

  r_ssize v_y_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_dimensions(...);

  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * 2];
  // interleave

  return rray_run_iterator(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides, 2);
}

Things I noticed while reading:

  • Speed: this shouldn't change. The strides are still built once per call, not once per run, and the iterator is still built inside the kernel. Your benchmarks showed that's the part that matters for speed.

  • The dimensionality check: rray_run_iterator() checks dimensionality only after copying into its fixed-size arrays. So the helper needs its own check_dimensionality() before filling the stack arrays. In return, the check this PR added to rray_binary_arithmetic_run() can go, because that function no longer fills any fixed-size array.

  • Broadcast and reduce: they have the same shape with one input. Broadcast uses (out dims, x dims) and reduce uses (x dims, out dims). One one-input helper would cover both. The early check_dimensionality() this PR added in rray_broadcast() could then go too, and rray_reduce_run() would stop building v_out_broadcast_strides.

  • Kernel signatures grow from 6 to 9 parameters. That will spread to all 84 binary kernels as they move over. Another option is rray_binary_run_iterator(x, y, v_dimensions, dimensionality), which reads r_dim(x) and r_dim(y) itself. rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_arrays() in src/strides.c already does this, and it already writes the interleaved layout. Kernels would then shrink to (x, y, v_dimensions, dimensionality, error_call). The cost is that the iterator header would read R attributes.

Before I write it, three questions:

  1. Do you want the plain integer version you wrote, or the version that takes x and y and gives shorter kernel signatures?

  2. Should I add the one-input helper for broadcast and reduce in the same change? If so, what should it be called? rray_unary_run_iterator() doesn't fit reduce well, because there x is the point space and out is the subspace.

  3. Should it live in strided-iterator2.h, next to rray_run_iterator()? It would need to include strides.h.

Davis

1 plain
2 not yet
3 I think binary.h

I added rray_binary_run_iterator() to src/binary.h. It compiles with no warnings and all 3497 tests pass. I haven't committed.

  • src/binary.h: the new static inline helper sits just above RRAY_BINARY_RUN(). It checks the dimensionality, fills the broadcast strides for x and y, interleaves them, and calls rray_run_iterator(). The macro now calls the helper. The header now also includes dimensionality.h and strides.h.

  • src/arithmetic.h: rray_binary_arithmetic_run_fn now takes the dimensions of x and y, each with its dimensionality, instead of v_strides.

  • src/arithmetic.c: rray_binary_arithmetic_run() no longer builds strides. It passes the dimensions straight through. I removed its check_dimensionality() call and the strides.h include, since nothing in it fills a fixed-size array any more.

  • src/arithmetic-add.c and src/decl/arithmetic-add-decl.h: all 16 add kernels have the new signature.

In the protection check, nothing new needed protecting. The dimension pointers come from x, y and dimensions, which the driver already protects. The kernel reads them before it allocates out.

I didn't re-run the benchmarks. The stride setup still happens once per call, inside the kernel, so I don't expect the speed to change.

Bash: sed -n 160,215p src/strided-iterator2.h; sed -n 85,150p src/binary.h; sed -n 160,200p src/bro… 
sed -n 160,215p src/strided-iterator2.h; sed -n 85,150p src/binary.h; sed -n 160,200p src/broadcast.c; sed -n 120,140p src/reduce.h; sed -n 85,125p src/arithmetic.c
  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
};

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides,
  r_ssize n
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
    r_stop_internal(
      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
      n,
      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
    );
  }

  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    r_lazy_null
  );

  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
  }

  r_memcpy(
    it.v_strides,
    v_strides,
    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)
  );

  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
    it.v_dimensions,
    it.v_strides,
    n,
    dimensionality
  );

  it.start = 0;
  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);

  }                                                                            \
                                                                               \
  FREE(1);                                                                     \
  return out;

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_binary_run_iterator(
  const int* v_x_dimensions,
  int x_dimensionality,
  const int* v_y_dimensions,
  int y_dimensionality,
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  r_ssize v_x_broadcast_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_dimensions(
    v_x_dimensions,
    x_dimensionality,
    dimensionality,
    v_x_broadcast_strides
  );

  r_ssize v_y_broadcast_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_dimensions(
    v_y_dimensions,
    y_dimensionality,
    dimensionality,
    v_y_broadcast_strides
  );

  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * 2];
  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    v_strides[axis * 2] = v_x_broadcast_strides[axis];
    v_strides[axis * 2 + 1] = v_y_broadcast_strides[axis];
  }

  return rray_run_iterator(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides, 2);
}

#define RRAY_BINARY_RUN(                                                       \
  X_CTYPE,                                                                     \
  X_CONST_DEREF,                                                               \
  X_CAST,                                                                      \
  Y_CTYPE,                                                                     \
  Y_CONST_DEREF,                                                               \
  Y_CAST,                                                                      \
  OUT_RTYPE,                                                                   \
  OUT_CTYPE,                                                                   \
  OUT_DEREF,                                                                   \
  ONE,                                                                         \
  ONE_ARGS                                                                     \
)                                                                              \
  struct rray_run_iterator it = rray_binary_run_iterator(                      \
    v_x_dimensions,                                                            \
    x_dimensionality,                                                          \
    v_y_dimensions,                                                            \
    y_dimensionality,                                                          \
    v_dimensions,                                                              \
    dimensionality                                                             \
  );                                                                           \
                                                                               \
  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(OUT_RTYPE, rray_run_iterator_size(&it)));   \
  OUT_CTYPE* v_out = OUT_DEREF(out);                                           \
                                                                               \
  const X_CTYPE* v_x = X_CONST_DEREF(x);                                       \
      }                                                                        \
    } else {                                                                   \
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                  \
        POKE(out, i, v_x[x_loc]);                                              \
        x_loc += x_stride;                                                     \
      }                                                                        \
    }                                                                          \
  }

#define RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC_POKE(OUT, I, VALUE) v_out[I] = (VALUE)

#define RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC(RTYPE, CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, DEREF)                \
  struct rray_run_iterator it =                                                \
    rray_run_iterator(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_broadcast_strides, 1); \
                                                                               \
  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(RTYPE, rray_run_iterator_size(&it)));       \
  CTYPE* v_out = DEREF(out);                                                   \
                                                                               \
  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                           \
                                                                               \
  RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP(CTYPE, RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC_POKE);                      \
                                                                               \
  FREE(1);                                                                     \
  return out;

#define RRAY_BROADCAST_BARRIER(RTYPE, CONST_DEREF, POKE)                       \
  struct rray_run_iterator it =                                                \
    rray_run_iterator(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_x_broadcast_strides, 1); \
                                                                               \
  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(RTYPE, rray_run_iterator_size(&it)));       \
                                                                               \

rray_run_iterator() now takes v_v_strides, one stride array per input, together with n. It builds its own axis-major v_strides in one loop that writes it.v_strides[axis * n + i] = v_v_strides[i][axis]. It compiles with no warnings and all 3497 tests pass. I haven't committed or pushed, since you approved that only for the last change.

  • src/strided-iterator2.h: the new loop replaces the old r_memcpy().

  • src/binary.h: I deleted rray_binary_run_iterator(). RRAY_BINARY_RUN() now puts v_x_broadcast_strides and v_y_broadcast_strides into a v_v_strides array and passes it to rray_run_iterator() with n = 2.

  • Kernels: the signature is now x, v_x_broadcast_strides, y, v_y_broadcast_strides, v_dimensions, dimensionality, error_call. I kept x's values next to x, as in your last ordering. This applies to the typedef, all 16 add kernels and their declarations.

  • src/arithmetic.c: rray_binary_arithmetic_run() fills the two stride arrays again. So it gets back its check_dimensionality() call and the strides.h include, because the stride arrays are fixed-size.

  • src/broadcast.c and src/reduce.h: the one-input callers now pass v_v_strides[] = {v_x_broadcast_strides} and {v_out_broadcast_strides}. I used the array form rather than &v_x_broadcast_strides so every caller looks the same.

You wrote v_x_strides and v_y_strides, but I named them v_x_broadcast_strides and v_y_broadcast_strides. That matches the names in broadcast and reduce, and your earlier rename commit. Tell me if you want the shorter names.

Davis

Commit and push

Claude

v_v_strides is now built in rray_binary_arithmetic_run(), right after the two broadcast stride arrays are filled. It's passed to the kernel, and RRAY_BINARY_RUN() uses it as is. It compiles with no warnings and all 3497 tests pass. Not committed.

  • src/arithmetic.c: it builds const r_ssize* v_v_strides[] = {v_x_broadcast_strides, v_y_broadcast_strides} and calls fn(x, y, v_v_strides, v_dimensions, dimensionality, error_call).

  • Kernels: the signature is now x, y, v_v_strides, v_dimensions, dimensionality, error_call, with v_v_strides typed const r_ssize* const*. This applies to the typedef, all 16 add kernels and their declarations.

  • src/binary.h: I removed the v_v_strides array from RRAY_BINARY_RUN().

Broadcast and reduce still build their one-input v_v_strides inside their macros. If you want them to match, the same move works for both: rray_broadcast() would build it after filling v_x_broadcast_strides, and rray_reduce_run() after filling v_out_broadcast_strides.

Both changes are in. They compile with no warnings and all 3497 tests pass. Not committed.

Broadcast and reduce:

  • rray_broadcast() builds v_v_strides[] = {v_x_broadcast_strides} right after filling the strides and passes it to the seven broadcast kernels. RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC() and RRAY_BROADCAST_BARRIER() use it as is.

  • rray_reduce_run() does the same with {v_out_broadcast_strides}. The rray_reduce_run_fn typedef and the six sum kernels now take const r_ssize* const* v_v_strides. RRAY_REDUCE_RUN() uses it as is.

  • In both, v_v_strides took the place of the old stride parameter, which was already right after the dimensionality.

Arithmetic order: the kernels now take x, y, v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_v_strides, error_call. That's the same order as rray_run_iterator(). The change covers the typedef, the 16 add kernels and their declarations, and the call in rray_binary_arithmetic_run().

r_obj* rray_reduce_run(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  bool na_rm,
  rray_reduce_run_fn_switch fn_switch,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, arg, error_call);
  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, arg, error_call));

  r_obj* x_dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions(x, arg, error_call));
  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);

  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  axes = KEEP(arg_as_axes(axes, dimensionality, rray_args.axes, error_call));
  const int* v_axes = r_int_cbegin(axes);
  const r_ssize axes_size = r_length(axes);

  r_obj* out_dimensions = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(dimensionality));
  int* v_out_dimensions = r_int_begin(out_dimensions);
  r_memcpy(v_out_dimensions, v_x_dimensions, sizeof(int) * dimensionality);
  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < axes_size; ++i) {
    v_out_dimensions[v_axes[i] - 1] = 1;
  }

  const r_ssize out_size =
    rray_size_from_dimensions(v_out_dimensions, dimensionality);

  r_ssize v_out_broadcast_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_dimensions(
    v_out_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    dimensionality,
    v_out_broadcast_strides
  );

  const r_ssize* v_v_strides[] = {v_out_broadcast_strides};

  const rray_reduce_run_fn fn = fn_switch(x, na_rm, arg, error_call);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(
    fn(x, out_size, v_x_dimensions, dimensionality, v_v_strides, error_call)
  );
  r_attrib_poke_dim(out, out_dimensions);

  r_obj* out_names = KEEP(rray_reduce_names(x, axes));

  if (out_names != r_null) {
    r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out, out_names);
  }

  FREE(6);
  return out;
}

r_obj* rray_reduce_nested(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* axes,
  bool na_rm,
  rray_reduce_nested_fn_switch fn_switch,
  struct rray_arg* arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call

Not as far as I can tell. That case was never measured, and I don't expect it to hurt speed. The benchmarks only compared two things:

  • Count passed as a literal (next(&it, 2), with everything inlined). This was as fast as the separate one-input and two-input iterators.

  • Count known only at runtime. Short runs were 25% to 75% slower (plans/iterators.md:19, section "Any number of inputs").

Storing n in the iterator while the constructor gets a literal 1 or 2 is a third case that wasn't tested.

Why I don't expect a slowdown. it is a local struct in the kernel, and both the constructor and next() are static inline. Once they're inlined, the compiler sees it.n = 2 written once and never changed, and its address never leaves the function. Splitting a struct into separate locals is an optimization both compilers do. After that, it.n in next() is just the constant 2, and the loops over inputs unroll as before.

Where it could go wrong:

  • The struct is about 34 KB. v_strides is 64 × 64 r_ssize. clang should still pull n out as its own value. GCC's version of this optimization has size limits, and I'm not certain how they apply here. GCC hasn't been checked for any of the iterator results.

  • Inlining is fragile. The plan already hit this once: a shared body that clang didn't inline meant the literal never reached the iterator. Today, if inlining fails, the n argument at each call site still says 2. With n stored, only the constructor's literal would do that job.

  • This is the same risk that sank the merged struct when the driver built it. If anyone builds the iterator outside the kernel and passes a pointer, n stops being a known constant again. But the run fields already behave that way, so storing n adds no new rule.

To settle it, I can store n, drop the argument from next(), and rerun the "Any number of inputs" benchmarks for broadcast, add and sum, with the short-run cases ([10, 1e6] + [10, 1]) included. Should I?

In the variant package, the existing one-input and two-input plans were repackaged into a `rray_strided_iterator_n_plan` (copying their already merged dimensions and strides into the axis-major stride layout) at the top of each function. A real version would build the n plan directly.

## Benchmarks

Two more versions were added to the variant package. Both use exactly the same loops as `run_split`:

- `run_n`: `rray_run_iterator_n` with a literal count, `1` for broadcast and sum, `2` for add.

- `run_n_runtime`: the same, with the count passed through a `volatile` variable so the compiler can't see its value. This stands in for code where the count is decided at runtime, like `rray_index()`.

Both pass the same 262 test expectations, and match `macro` with `identical()` in every case.

The first attempt shared one function body between the two versions, with each wrapper passing its count. clang didn't inline that body into the literal wrapper, so the literal never reached the iterator, and `run_n` was as slow as `run_n_runtime`. Marking the shared body `__attribute__((always_inline))` fixed it. In real code each function calls the iterator directly with its literal, which is what the first `run_n` measurement did, and it matched `run_split` without any forced inlining. But it shows the speed depends on the compiler actually seeing the literal.

Median ms, with inlining forced:

| Case | Macro | Run split | Run n | Run n, runtime count |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 6.24 | 6.38 | 6.45 | 8.07 |
| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 6.68 | 7.12 | 7.25 | 8.11 |
| Broadcast `[200, 1, 200]` to `[200, 200, 200]` | 4.05 | 4.13 | 4.05 | 4.37 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 7.69 | 8.33 | 8.71 | 10.76 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 7.24 | 7.68 | 7.92 | 10.25 |
| Add, integer, `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 10.97 | 11.57 | 12.08 | 14.89 |
| Add, integer, `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 13.23 | 13.84 | 13.54 | 15.68 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.53 | 2.65 | 2.93 | 4.68 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 3.79 | 3.75 | 3.75 | 5.23 |
| Sum `[200, 200, 200]` over 2 | 1.18 | 1.18 | 1.18 | 1.28 |

Every other case in the suite (long runs: the `[4000, 4000]` and most `[200, 200, 200]` cases) was the same for all four versions, within noise.

With `RRAY_MAX_INPUTS` raised to 64, so `rray_index()` could use it, `run_n` was unchanged: for example 2.84 ms on the short sum along axis 1 and 6.16 ms on the short broadcast. The compiler still keeps the used slots in registers.

## What the numbers say

- With a literal count, one iterator for any number of inputs is as fast as the separate one-input and two-input versions. The small differences between `run_split` and `run_n` move in both directions between runs.

- A count known only at runtime costs 25% to 75% on short runs, and nothing on long runs. The loops over inputs run once per run, so they only matter when there are millions of runs.

- So the plan structs can be merged into one, as long as every function passes its count as a literal. Code like `rray_index()`, where the count really is decided at runtime, keeps its `switch` on the count, with a literal in each case.

---

# Removing the per-run cost

`rray_run_iterator_n` does two things per run that the macros don't:

- It keeps two location arrays. `v_loc_start` is what `next()` advances, and `v_loc` is a copy made at the start of every run for the caller. The copy only protects against a caller that changes `it.v_loc[i]` while walking the run. No caller does: every loop copies the start into a local (`r_ssize x_loc = it.v_loc[0];`) and moves the local.

- `next()` checks `it->end != 0` on every run, so that the first call doesn't advance.

## Two leaner iterators

`rray_run_iterator_n_lean` keeps one array, `v_loc`, which `next()` advances directly. The caller reads it and never writes it. C can't enforce that, since the iterator itself has to change the field. It still has the first-run check.

156	  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
157	  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
158	  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
159	  int dimensionality;
160	
161	  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
162	  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
163	  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
164	};
165	
166	static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
167	  const int* v_dimensions,
168	  int dimensionality,
169	  const r_ssize* const* v_v_strides,
170	  r_ssize n
171	) {
172	  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);
173	
174	  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
175	    r_stop_internal(
176	      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
177	      n,
178	      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
179	    );
180	  }
181	
182	  struct rray_run_iterator it;
183	
184	  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
185	    v_dimensions,
186	    dimensionality,
187	    r_lazy_null
188	  );
189	
190	  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
191	    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
192	  }
193	
194	  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
195	    r_ssize* v_strides = it.v_strides + axis * n;
196	    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
197	      v_strides[i] = v_v_strides[i][axis];
198	    }
199	  }
200	
201	  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
202	    it.v_dimensions,
203	    it.v_strides,
204	    n,
205	    dimensionality
206	  );
207	
208	  it.start = 0;
209	  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];
210	
211	  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
212	    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
213	  }
214	
215	  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);
216	
217	  return it;
218	}
219	
220	static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_size(
221	  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
222	) {
223	  return it->size;
224	}
225	
226	static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_start(
227	  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
228	) {
229	  return it->start;
230	}
231	
232	static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_end(
233	  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
234	) {
235	  return it->end;
236	}
237	
238	static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_loc(
239	  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,
240	  r_ssize i
241	) {
242	  return it->v_loc[i];
243	}
244	
245	static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_stride(
246	  const struct rray_run_iterator* it,
247	  r_ssize i
248	) {
249	  return it->v_strides[i];
250	}
251	
252	static inline bool rray_run_iterator_done(const struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
253	  return it->start == it->size;
254	}
255	
256	static inline void rray_run_iterator_next(
257	  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
258	  r_ssize n
259	) {
260	  for (int axis = 1; axis < it->dimensionality; ++axis) {
261	    const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides + axis * n;
262	    const r_ssize dimension = it->v_dimensions[axis];
263	
264	    ++it->v_point[axis];
265	
266	    if (it->v_point[axis] < dimension) {
267	      for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
268	        it->v_loc[i] += v_strides[i];
269	      }
270	      break;
271	    }
272	
273	    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
274	
275	    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
276	      it->v_loc[i] -= (dimension - 1) * v_strides[i];
277	    }
278	  }
279	
280	  it->start = it->end;

Clang doesn't inline the constructor, so it.n ends up loaded from the stack and next() compiles into a general, vectorized loop over n. I'll check the baseline and GCC output for the same pattern.

The compiler doesn't inline the constructor in either version—baseline still unrolls next() into two msubs per axis since it sees the literal 2, but the variant can't. I'll check GCC's handling of the call next.

GCC clones the constructor with n fixed but still calls it out of line, so the caller never sees the constant. I'll add two more builds that force the constructor inline via __attribute__((always_inline)), giving 4 versions × 2 compilers.

Thinking

n is still being reloaded from the stack after the R calls, suggesting it's address leaks—likely via the r_memset() call on it.v_point, which compiles to a real ___memset_chk. I'll test this with assembly-only experiments.

Found it: clang's optimization remarks show the compiler reloads it->n each time in next() because it can't prove the runtime-indexed writes to v_loc[i] don't clobber it. Now checking the R function signatures before looking at timings.

lib <- args[[1]]
tag <- args[[2]]
round <- args[[3]]
out <- args[[4]]

library(rray4, lib.loc = lib)

set.seed(1)

dbl <- function(...) array(runif(prod(c(...))), c(...))
int <- function(...) array(sample(100L, prod(c(...)), TRUE), c(...))
lgl <- function(...) array(sample(c(TRUE, FALSE), prod(c(...)), TRUE), c(...))
chr <- function(...) array(sample(letters, prod(c(...)), TRUE), c(...))
with_na <- function(x) {
  x[sample(length(x), length(x) %/% 100)] <- NA
  x
}

m <- dbl(4000, 4000)
cube <- dbl(200, 200, 200)
wide <- dbl(10, 1e6)
narrow <- dbl(2, 4e6)
four <- dbl(10, 10, 10, 1e4)

  "sum dbl [10, 10, 10, 1e4] over 2, 4" = \() rray_sum(four, c(2L, 4L)),
  "sum dbl na_rm [10, 1e6] over 1" = \() rray_sum(wide_na, 1L, na_rm = TRUE),
  "sum dbl na_rm [4000, 4000] over 2" = \() rray_sum(m_na, 2L, na_rm = TRUE),
  "sum int [10, 1e6] over 1" = \() rray_sum(wide_i, 1L),
  "sum int [10, 1e6] over 2" = \() rray_sum(wide_i, 2L),
  "sum lgl [10, 1e6] over 1" = \() rray_sum(wide_l, 1L)
)

res <- lapply(names(cases), function(name) {
  fn <- cases[[name]]
  fn()
  b <- bench::mark(fn(), min_time = 0.4, min_iterations = 10, check = FALSE)
  times <- as.numeric(b$time[[1]])
  data.frame(
    case = name,
    tag = tag,
    round = round,
    median_ms = median(times) * 1e3,
    min_ms = min(times) * 1e3,
    n = length(times)
  )
})

write.csv(do.call(rbind, res), out, row.names = FALSE)

S <- commandArgs(trailingOnly = TRUE)[[1]]

files <- list.files(file.path(S, "results"), full.names = TRUE)

names(wide) <- sub("median_ms.", "", names(wide), fixed = TRUE)
wide <- wide[match(unique(df$case), wide$case), ]

spread <- aggregate(median_ms ~ case + tag, df, \(x) (max(x) - min(x)) / median(x))
noise <- tapply(spread$median_ms, spread$tag, median)

fmt <- function(x) formatC(x, format = "f", digits = 2)
pct <- function(a, b) sprintf("%+.0f%%", (a / b - 1) * 100)

  v <- wide[[paste0("var_", cc)]]

Any other bright ideas on how to ensure that next loop gets unrolled?

Claude

I have two ideas, and one of them can be tested in a few minutes on the assembly alone, so I'll check it first.

Idea: put n first in the struct. The remarks showed n is lost because of the store to it->v_loc[i] at a runtime i, and v_loc sits before n. Every runtime index in the iterator is at least 0. If n comes before all the arrays, no write at a non-negative index into those arrays can reach it, and clang's alias analysis can reason about exactly that.

Putting n first in the struct doesn't help. The variant code stays the same size as when n was stored after v_loc, with clang and GCC, inlined or not. So any approach where next() reads n back out of the struct is out. What's left is to have the literal written into the code that calls next().

  1. Put the count in the function name. Add rray_run_iterator_next1(&it) and rray_run_iterator_next2(&it). Each is a one-line wrapper that calls a shared rray__run_iterator_next(it, n) with 1 or 2. Call sites never mention n, but the literal sits right next to the loop. Mark the shared body __attribute__((always_inline)). Your plan already saw clang skip inlining a shared body once, and that loses the literal just as storing n does. The constructor could follow the same pattern (rray_run_iterator2(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_v_strides)). This is the smallest change.

  2. Put the count in the type. Make struct rray_run_iterator1 and struct rray_run_iterator2 with arrays sized for exactly 1 or 2 inputs, each with its own next(). Passing a one-input iterator to the two-input next() becomes a compile error. Code where the count is only known at runtime, like rray_index(), would still need the general version or a switch. You'd write one struct per count, or generate them with a macro.

    This also fixes something I noticed in the assembly. The current struct is about 34 KB, mostly the 64 × 64 v_strides array. That size makes every kernel call ___chkstk_darwin to probe the stack on entry. A two-input struct is about 2.5 KB and wouldn't need that.

I'd go with option 1 if the goal is only to stop passing n at the call sites. Option 2 is worth it if you also want the smaller stack and the type check. Option 2 can be built on top of option 1 later without changing the loops in the kernels.

Davis

Let’s go with a variant of option 1

  • rray_run_iterator1(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides)
  • rray_run_iterator2(v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides1, v_strides2)

Those handle the merging of strides and handoff to rray run iterator

  • rray_run_iterator_next1/2 as you mentioned, but they don’t call the underlying next, they are their own loops that manually unroll, to ensure that it happens!

Claude

166	static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
167	  const int* v_dimensions,
168	  int dimensionality,
169	  const r_ssize* const* v_v_strides,
170	  r_ssize n
171	) {
172	  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);
173	
174	  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
175	    r_stop_internal(
176	      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
177	      n,
178	      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
179	    );
180	  }
181	
182	  struct rray_run_iterator it;
183	
184	  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
185	    v_dimensions,
186	    dimensionality,
187	    r_lazy_null
188	  );
189	
190	  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
191	    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
192	  }
193	
194	  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
195	    r_ssize* v_strides = it.v_strides + axis * n;
196	    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
197	      v_strides[i] = v_v_strides[i][axis];
198	    }
199	  }
200	
201	  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
202	    it.v_dimensions,
203	    it.v_strides,
204	    n,
205	    dimensionality
206	  );
207	
208	  it.start = 0;
209	  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];
210	
211	  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
212	    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
213	  }
214	
215	  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);
216	
217	  return it;
218	}
219

221	  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
222	) {

224	}

247	  r_ssize i

252	static inline bool rray_run_iterator_done(const struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
253	  return it->start == it->size;
254	}
255	
256	static inline void rray_run_iterator_next(
257	  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
258	  r_ssize n
259	) {
260	  for (int axis = 1; axis < it->dimensionality; ++axis) {
261	    const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides + axis * n;
262	    const r_ssize dimension = it->v_dimensions[axis];
263	
264	    ++it->v_point[axis];
265	
266	    if (it->v_point[axis] < dimension) {
267	      for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
268	        it->v_loc[i] += v_strides[i];
269	      }
270	      break;
271	    }
272	
273	    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
274	
275	    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
276	      it->v_loc[i] -= (dimension - 1) * v_strides[i];
277	    }
278	  }
279	
280	  it->start = it->end;
281	  it->end += it->v_dimensions[0];
282	}

285	  r_ssize* v_dimensions,
286	  r_ssize* v_strides,
287	  r_ssize n,
288	  int dimensionality

+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_next1(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
+  for (int axis = 1; axis < it->dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    const r_ssize stride = it->v_strides[axis];
+    const r_ssize dimension = it->v_dimensions[axis];
+
+    ++it->v_point[axis];
+
+    if (it->v_point[axis] < dimension) {
+      it->v_loc[0] += stride;
+      break;
+    }
+
+    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
+
+    it->v_loc[0] -= (dimension - 1) * stride;
+  }
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end += it->v_dimensions[0];
+}
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_next2(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
+  for (int axis = 1; axis < it->dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides + axis * 2;
+    const r_ssize stride1 = v_strides[0];
+    const r_ssize stride2 = v_strides[1];
+    const r_ssize dimension = it->v_dimensions[axis];
+
+    ++it->v_point[axis];
+
+    if (it->v_point[axis] < dimension) {
+      it->v_loc[0] += stride1;
+      it->v_loc[1] += stride2;
+      break;
+    }
+
+    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
+
+    it->v_loc[0] -= (dimension - 1) * stride1;
+    it->v_loc[1] -= (dimension - 1) * stride2;
+  }
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end += it->v_dimensions[0];
+}

Thinking

GCC's per-function numbers aren't trustworthy here since my extractor overran into neighboring functions, but the key signal is clear: GCC now hoists iterator fields like size and dimensionality into registers before the loop instead of reloading them each run. I'll run a quick timing check on the short-run cases to confirm this shows up in practice.

args <- commandArgs(trailingOnly = TRUE)
library(rray4, lib.loc = args[[1]])
set.seed(1)
dbl <- function(...) array(runif(prod(c(...))), c(...))
int <- function(...) array(sample(100L, prod(c(...)), TRUE), c(...))
wide <- dbl(10, 1e6); narrow <- dbl(2, 4e6); wide_i <- int(10, 1e6); four <- dbl(10, 10, 10, 1e4)
y1 <- dbl(10, 1); y2 <- dbl(1, 1e6); y3 <- dbl(2, 1); yi <- int(10, 1); y4 <- dbl(10, 1, 10, 1)
x1 <- dbl(10, 1); x2 <- dbl(2, 1)
cases <- list(
  "broadcast [10, 1] to [10, 1e6]" = \() rray_broadcast(x1, c(10L, 1e6L)),
  "broadcast [2, 1] to [2, 4e6]" = \() rray_broadcast(x2, c(2L, 4e6L)),
  "add [10, 1e6] + [10, 1]" = \() rray_add(wide, y1),
  "add [10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]" = \() rray_add(wide, y2),
  "add [2, 4e6] + [2, 1]" = \() rray_add(narrow, y3),
  "add int [10, 1e6] + [10, 1]" = \() rray_add(wide_i, yi),
  "add [10, 10, 10, 1e4] + [10, 1, 10, 1]" = \() rray_add(four, y4),
  "sum [10, 1e6] over 1" = \() rray_sum(wide, 1L),
  "sum [10, 1e6] over 2" = \() rray_sum(wide, 2L),
  "sum [2, 4e6] over 1" = \() rray_sum(narrow, 1L)
)
for (nm in names(cases)) {
  b <- bench::mark(cases[[nm]](), min_time = 0.5, min_iterations = 10, check = FALSE)
  cat(sprintf("%s\t%s\t%.2f\n", args[[2]], nm, median(as.numeric(b$time[[1]])) * 1e3))
}

Davis

Doesn’t R on Mac only use clang? Why check gcc at all?

Claude

You're right that it doesn't matter for Mac. CRAN's macOS builds use Apple clang, so your Mac users never run GCC-built code.

It matters elsewhere. R on Linux builds packages with GCC by default, and Rtools on Windows is GCC too. So most rray4 users outside macOS would run GCC-built code. That's why I checked it. Two caveats:

  • My GCC numbers come from GCC 15 on arm64 macOS. That's close to what Linux users get, but not identical: different CPU, often an older GCC.

  • For this change, GCC doesn't change the answer. next1() and next2() have no loop over inputs to unroll, so every compiler sees the same straight-line code.

If you're happy to rely on clang for these decisions, I'll drop GCC and run the short-run timing check with clang only. Or I can skip the timing, since the clang assembly is already within a few instructions of the baseline.

Davis

Davis

Do you think it is better to remove all of the run iterator accessor functions to avoid the potential &it pointer being created and messing something up with compiler optimizations if it isn’t inlined?

Claude

Bash: S=/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e… 
S=/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e269db/scratchpad/nbench/asm && grep -cE '\bbl\s+_?rray_run_iterator_(size|start|end|loc|stride|done|next[12]?)\b' $S/new-*.s
/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e269db/scratchpad/nbench/asm/new-clang-broadcast.s:0
/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e269db/scratchpad/nbench/asm/new-gcc-15-arithmetic-add.s:0
/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e269db/scratchpad/nbench/asm/new-clang-reduce-sum.s:0
/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e269db/scratchpad/nbench/asm/new-clang-arithmetic-add.s:0
/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e269db/scratchpad/nbench/asm/new-gcc-15-reduce-sum.s:0
/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/2e0d3c64-5425-46da-904a-f441e8e269db/scratchpad/nbench/asm/new-gcc-15-broadcast.s:0

No, I'd keep them. Removing the accessors wouldn't get rid of &it, and they aren't causing any problem now.

  • They're always inlined. In the assembly for the current commit, there's no call to any accessor, rray_run_iterator_done(), next1() or next2() in broadcast, add or sum, with clang or GCC. One-line static inline functions are about the safest thing to inline, so I don't expect that to change.

  • &it exists anyway. rray_run_iterator_next1(&it) and rray_run_iterator_done(&it) take its address too. Reading it.start directly would still leave next1(&it) in every loop, so removing the accessors buys nothing.

  • it lives in memory for other reasons. The constructor isn't inlined, so it writes the 34 KB struct to the stack through a hidden pointer. And next1() / next2() write v_point[axis] and v_loc[...], which makes the compiler reload fields after those writes. That's the same effect that sank the stored n. Field reads compile to the same loads with or without accessors.

  • The case that hurts is different. Your plan's bad case was a large function that wasn't inlined receiving &it: the driver built the iterator and the kernel used it through a pointer. The accessors aren't like that. If one ever failed to inline, the fix would be __attribute__((always_inline)) on that accessor, not removing them all.

So the accessors cost nothing in the generated code, and they keep v_strides and v_loc out of the kernel macros. Those two layouts are exactly the details that just changed twice.

Davis

“which makes the compiler reload fields after those writes” is there any way to avoid that out of curiosity

Claude

Yes, a few ways, but the measured benefit has been about zero, so I wouldn't change anything.

The cause in one line: next1() and next2() write it->v_point[axis] with an axis known only at runtime. The compiler can't prove that write stays inside v_point, so it assumes the write might have changed any other field of it. After each run it reloads size, dimensionality, v_dimensions[0] and so on.

  1. Split the struct into fixed and changing parts. Keep size, dimensionality, v_dimensions and v_strides in one local struct, and start, end, v_loc and v_point in another. They're two separate objects on the stack, and the compiler knows a write into one can never touch the other. So the fixed fields can stay in registers for the whole loop. This is the reliable fix, and it's essentially the old design: a plan of fixed state plus positions managed by the caller. The header comment from that era says this is why it was split. You merged them in 6cc1e10 after your benchmarks showed the merged struct, built inside the kernel, was as fast.

  2. Copy fields into locals at the top of next1() / next2(). For example, const int dimensionality = it->dimensionality;. That removes reloads within one call, but not across runs, because the next call reads the fields again after the previous call's writes. It's a small gain at best.

  3. Tell the compiler the indices stay in range. For example, if (axis >= RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY) __builtin_unreachable();. In principle that lets it prove the write stays inside v_point. The test with n first in the struct used the same kind of reasoning (non-negative indices can't reach a field placed before the arrays), and clang didn't use it. I wouldn't count on this.

Why it barely matters: the reloads happen once per run, not once per element, and they hit L1 cache. They only show up when runs are tiny, like [2, 4e6]. Even there, the merged iterator stayed within a few percent of the old macros in your plan's measurements. Option 1 is the one to reach for if a short-run benchmark ever shows a real gap.
